Natura, Olver, and Végh [17] developed a general technique. They introduced a strongly polynomial rounding
step using Caratheodory decomposition arguments to convert a point in the central path neighborhood to one of
bounded bit-complexity. Further, this is applicable irrespective of the particular iteration used by the algorithm.
Their rounding technique can be used for a broad class of path following IPMs, but it is computationally expensive.
    The TR2-Solve(B, b, I, J, δ) algorithm can be easily implemented in the Turing model: the main computations
are a Cholesky decomposition, and solving linear systems. It is also easy to argue that the value of the parameter
λ, obtained using binary search and Newton’s method, remains polynomially bounded in the input.
    In contrast, the TRW-Solve(B, b, I, J, δ) algorithm relies on the Robust IPM [71] and hence the claimed
running time holds in the Real RAM model. This is also the case for the IPM algorithms TRW-IPM and TR2-
IPM. Even though every iteration is strongly polynomial, the linear systems to compute the step-lengths involve the
current iterate and thus the bit complexity could double in every O(1) iterations. These could also be converted to
algorithms in the Turing model using the rounding steps from [17], but this would involve a running time overhead.

Approximating square roots. Square-root computations are not allowed in the real RAM (or Turing) model;
this is particularly challenging since IPMs often require them, particularly, for computing norms. In our algorithms,
we also require Euclidean norms of vectors (see e.g., Lemma 4.7 and Lemma 5.5). However, we do not need exact
values (and not even very high accuracy approximations). We can approximate vector norms in strongly polynomial
time using Newton’s method, and the observation that ∥x∥1 gives a rough starting approximation of ∥x∥.

Proposition
    √           2.1 (Square
                         √ root computation). For any given x > 0 and ε > 0, suppose a > 0 is given such that
a ≤ x, it takes√O (log ( x/a) +√log log(1/ε)) many arithmetic operations in strongly polynomial time to compute
λ that satisfies x ≤ λ ≤ (1 + ε) x.
   For computing
           √      the norm ∥x∥ for x ∈ Rn , we can compute a starting estimate ∥x∥1 using basic arithmetic. Noting
that ∥x∥1 / n ≤ ∥x∥ ≤ ∥x∥1 , and starting with a multiplicative binary search (see Algorithm 5), then Newton’s
method yields the following corollary.
Proposition 2.2 (ℓ2 -computation). For any given x ∈ Rn and ε > 0, we can compute some λ that satisfies
∥x∥ ≤ λ ≤ (1 + ε)∥x∥ in strongly polynomial time with O (log log(n) + log log (1/ε)) many operations.
  In Appendix C, we show how a pivoted Cholesky factorization can be implemented in the square-root free real
RAM model, avoiding approximate square roots during a standard Cholesky factorization.

2.3    Preliminaries on interior point methods
In (LP), the program in x will be referred to as the primal problem and the program in (y, s) as the dual program.
Recall the notation

                   P := {x ∈ Rn : Ax = b, x ≥ 0} ,          D := {s ∈ Rn : ∃y s.t. A⊤ y + s = c, s ≥ 0}

for the primal and dual feasible regions, and P>0 := {x ∈ P : x > 0}, D>0 := {s ∈ D : s > 0}. Throughout, we
assume that (LP) is bounded and strictly feasible, so that P>0 , D>0 ̸= ∅, and also n ≥ 2. For the following two
simple propositions, see e.g. [5].

Proposition 2.3. Given z = (x, s), z ′ = (x′ , s′ ) ∈ P × D, we have

                                         ⟨x, s⟩ + ⟨x′ , s′ ⟩ = ⟨x, s′ ⟩ + ⟨x′ , s⟩ .

Lemma 2.9. For θ ∈ [0, 1), let z = (x, s) ∈ N −∞ (θ) with µ(z) = µ and z1 = (x1 , s1 ) ∈ N −∞ (θ) with µ(z1 ) = γµ
where γ ∈ [0, 1]. Then, [z1 , z] ⊆ N −∞ (1 − γ(1 − θ)).
Proof. Let λ ∈ [0, 1]. By Proposition 2.4, µ((1 − λ)z + λz1 ) = (1 − λ)µ + λγµ. Using the AM-GM inequality,
                            ((1 − λ)x + λx1 )((1 − λ)s + λs1 ) ≥ (xs)1−λ (x1 s1 )λ
                                                                      ≥ (1 − θ)µ1−λ (γµ)λ
                                                                      ≥ (1 − θ)γµ
                                                                      ≥ (1 − θ)γµ((1 − λ)z + λz1 ) .
Hence, [z1 , z] ⊆ N −∞ (1 − γ(1 − θ)).

                                                                 16
2.4    Predictor-corrector step
Given z = (x, s) ∈ P>0 × D>0 , the search directions commonly used in interior-point methods are obtained as the
solution (∆x, ∆s) ∈ ker(A) × Im(A⊤ ) to the following linear system for some ν ∈ [0, 1].

                                                       A∆x = 0                                                       (10)
                                                  ⊤
                                               A ∆y + ∆s = 0                                                         (11)
                                                 s∆x + x∆s = νµ1 − xs                                                (12)

Predictor-corrector methods, such as the Mizuno–Todd–Ye Predictor-Corrector Algorithm [50], alternate between
two types of steps. In corrector steps, we use ν = 1, which gives the centrality direction, denoted as ∆z c = (∆xc , ∆sc )
throughout. In predictor steps, we use ν = 0. This direction is also called the affine scaling direction, and will
be denoted as ∆z a = (∆xa , ∆sa ) throughout. The following proposition summarizes the well-known properties of
predictor and corrector steps.
Proposition 2.10. For β ∈ (0, 1), let z = (x, s) ∈ N 2 (β).
                                                                                                 √   2
                                                                                                  2β
  (i) Let ∆z c be the centrality direction at z. Then µ(z + ∆z c ) = µ(z) and z + ∆z c ∈ N 2 ( 4(1−β) ).

 (ii) Let α ∈ [0, 1] and ∆z a be the affine scaling direction at z, then µ(z + α∆z a ) = (1 − α)µ(z). Moreover, if
      z + α∆z a ∈ N 2 (β), then z + α′ ∆z a ∈ N 2 (β) for any α′ ∈ [0, α].

(iii) For any k > 0 such that (k + 1)β < 1, let α := sup α ∈ [0, 1] : z + α′ ∆z a ∈ N 2 ((k + 1)β), ∀ α′ ∈ [0, α] . We
                                                           

      define α as follows: if ∥∆xa ∆sa ∥ = 0, α := 1, otherwise let α be the root on R>0 of

                                                    α2 ∆xa ∆sa
                                                                          = kβ .                                     (13)
                                                   1−α   µ
               √
      Then, kβ/ n ≤ α ≤ α.

Proof. For the first parts of (i) and(ii), see e.g. [50]. The second part of (i) is a result of [49, Lemma 1], and the
second part of (ii) is [4, Lemma 2]. (iii) can be derived analogously following Lemma 4.17 and Theorem 4.18 in [78].


   The following proposition explicitly states that the predictor and corrector steps can be computed in strongly
polynomial time, because computing the steps amounts to solving linear systems based on the data x, s, µ and ν.

t ∈ Rn . Then, the solution to s∆x + x∆s = t, ∆x ∈ ker(A) and ∆s ∈ Im(A⊤ ) can be expressed as

Moreover, both the affine scaling step (∆xa , ∆sa ) and corrector step (∆xc , ∆sc ), corresponding to t = νµ1n − xs
for ν ∈ {0, 1} respectively can be computed in strongly polynomial time.

2.5    Curvature of a straight line
We next prove the two lemmas mentioned in Section 1.4.1. Given two points z = (x, s), z1 = (x1 , s1 ) ∈ N 2 (β) with
µ(z) = µ and µ(z1 ) = µ1 , µ1 ≤ µ, we recall from (3) the curvature of the straight line between z1 and z, defined as

                                                          ∥(x1 − x)(s1 − s)∥

This quantity determines how close the straight line [z1 , z] is to the central path in the ℓ2 -neighborhood, by the
following lemma. The final part shows that the curvature between central path points gives a tight bound on the
neighborhood containing the line segment between these points, which plays a key role in proving Theorem 1.7.

assumption z, z1 ∈ N 2 (β) to bound ξˆ12 − 1 , ξˆ2 − 1 ≤ β.

2.6    Local proximity of points in ℓ2 -neighborhood

2.7    Fast IPM solvers
We now state the properties of the Robust IPM by van den Brand [71] and show how it can be used in our algorithm.
The following potential is used to define the central path neighborhood in these methods. We use the version as
described in the tutorial [43]. Let Φ : Rn → R be defined as
                                                   n                      n

Lemma 2.21. Let x, s ∈ Rn>0 , t ≥ 0. If Φ(xs/t − 1n ) ≤ 16n, then ∥xs/t − 1n ∥∞ ≤ 1/16, and consequently,
(x, s) ∈ N −∞ (1/8).
Proof. For every i ∈ [n], 21 eλ|ri | ≤ Φ(r), and therefore ∥r∥∞ ≤ log(2Φ(r))/λ. The first claim follows by the choice
of λ. This in particular implies |µ(z)/t − 1| ≤ 1/16 for z = (x, s), implying z ∈ N −∞ (1/8).
   For the following theorem from [71], see [43, Lemma 24].

P × D and t̄ > 0 such that Φ(x̄s̄/t̄ − 1n ) ≤ 16n, and ϱ ∈ (0, 1), outputs (xout , sout ) ∈ P × D and tout ≤ ϱt such that

   Next, we show how to use the Robust IPM method to approximately solve LP with ℓ∞ -norm constraint.
Definition 2.24 (Approximate ℓ∞ -regression solver). An approximate ℓ∞ regression solver is an algorithm that,
given a matrix B ∈ Rm×n , a vector b ∈ Im(B), and a parameter δ > 0, either
  (i) returns a vector y ∈ Rn with By = b and ∥y∥∞ ≤ 1 + δ, or
 (ii) returns a vector z ∈ Rm with ∥B⊤ z∥1 < ⟨b, z⟩, certifying that no vector y with By = b and ∥y∥∞ ≤ 1 exists.
    Note that the second outcome is a Farkas certificate. In particular, for any y with By = b we have that
⟨b, z⟩ = y ⊤ B⊤ z ≤ ∥y∥∞ ∥B⊤ z∥1 < ∥y∥∞ ⟨b, z⟩, showing ∥y∥∞ > 1.
Theorem 2.25. There exists  an approximate ℓ∞ -regression solver LMFeas(B, b, δ) satisfying Definition 2.24 that
runs in time O nω̃+o(1) log 1δ .
                                                                                                               †
    The proof
            √ is given in Appendix A; we give a brief sketch. We first compute the minimum-norm point ȳ = B b.
If ∥ȳ∥2 > n, we may conclude infeasibility, and find a Farkas certificate as in (ii). Otherwise, we can use ȳ to
initialize the IPM for min α s.t. By = b, ∥y∥∞ ≤ 1 + α with α = O(n).


                                                                  22
2.8    Singular value decompositions
The eigenvalue problem has always been of great interest and has been extensively studied in different communities.
Various algorithms with different approaches, computational models and notions of approximation were proposed
to tackle this problem, see e.g. [8, 9, 10, 57]. In particular, the randomized algorithm in [9] achieves diagonalization
of general matrices with high probability in nearly matrix multiplication time. For general non-Hermitian matrices,
it is difficult to design a globally convergent and numerically stable algorithm to approximate the eigenvalues and
eigenvectors; see [19]. In contrast, fast and stable eigenvalue algorithms that globally converge are well-known for
Hermitian matrices (e.g. [75]) with further speed-up possible, for example, Õ(nω ) running time if only the largest
eigenvalue is needed [44].
     For our purpose to compute the critical points (see Section 1.4.3), we require an algorithm to approximate only
the eigenvalues of a symmetric positive semi-definite matrix. However, as opposed to the above algorithms, such
approximation needs to be multiplicative, and the algorithm needs to be implementable in strongly polynomial time
in order to fit in our overall computation model. The requirement is captured by the following definition.
Definition 2.26 (Partial SVD). Let A ∈ Rm×n with m ≤ n. A matrix U ∈ Rrk(A)×n is called a partial ϱ-SVD of
A for some parameter ϱ ≥ 1 if U has orthogonal rows and for all x ∈ Rn we have that
                                           ϱ−1 ∥Ux∥2 ≤ ∥Ax∥2 ≤ ϱ∥Ux∥2 .                                            (26)
    Our definition of a partial SVD resembles a spectral approximation with the additional requirement of an
orthogonal basis whose basis vectors correspond approximately to singular vectors of A and whose basis vector
norms correspond approximately to the singular values of A.
    Since the development of Francis’s QR algorithm, there is a long line of research on the use of rank-revealing
QR decomposition via column pivoting to multiplicatively approximate the eigenvalues. One of the earliest such
algorithms was proposed by Chan [11], then algorithms with tighter approximation and better implementation were
developed in [12, 30, 56]. However, these algorithms generally require the computation of Householder reflectors or
a certain singular vector, which cannot be done in strongly polynomial time. More recently, a randomized strongly
polynomial algorithm was provided by Diakonikolas, Tzamos and Kane [22] to compute a (1 + ε)-approximate
singular value decomposition, by running power iterations on a randomized initial basis. Allamigeon, Dadush,
Loho, Natura, and Végh in [5] also gave a deterministic algorithm for (1 + ε)-singular value decomposition which
was needed in the subroutine of their IPM. Their algorithm is based on Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization with a
special pivoting rule which replaces the role of Householder reflectors, and has a strongly polynomial Õ(n5 ) running
time. While strongly polynomial, the running time of their algorithm is significantly inferior to that of the weakly
polynomial algorithms commonly referenced in the literature. Their approach can be characterized as a pivoted
QR orthogonalization, wherein the primary computational bottleneck arises from the pivoting step, the selection
of which requires complexity of order Õ(n4 ). The substantial computational overhead associated with pivoting
emerges from the necessity, at each iteration, to compute projections of every column yet to be pivoted onto the
orthogonal complement of the set of remaining columns.
    It is noteworthy that their algorithm could alternatively be reformulated through repeated matrix inversion,
incurring a computational cost of Õ(nω ) per iteration. Nevertheless, achieving a running time better than Õ(nω+1 )
via this matrix inversion strategy appears unlikely.
    In Appendix C.1, we prove Lemma C.1 by presenting a O(n3 ) variant of the algorithm in [5], using pivoted
Cholesky factorization. It gives a multiplicative ϱ = O(n22n )-approximation of the eigenvalues of a symmetric
positive definite matrix in strongly polynomial time. The running time is comparable to standard QR, Cholesky
and LDLT factorizations, if no fast matrix multiplication is used. In particular, at each iteration, the algorithm
applies the Schur complement computation in the row and column with largest diagonal entry. Beyond operating
on the symmetric Gram matrix, the primary conceptual distinction from [5] lies in the pivot selection criterion:
our method selects pivots according to descending singular values, whereas [5] selects pivots based on ascending
singular values.
    Although one could boost this result and get an (1 + ε)-approximation by applying this algorithm to a power of
the matrix, we note that already the weak approximation suffices for our purposes, as the running time dependence
on ϱ in TR2-Solve(B, b, I, J, δ) is log log ϱ (see Lemma 7.5).
    For the ℓ∞ -regression problem (Section 1.4.4) however, we also do require approximations to the singular vectors.
To this end, we show in Appendix C the following result, which is indeed achieved by boosting the method of
Lemma C.1 via matrix powers.
Lemma 2.27 (Deterministic strongly polynomial partial SVD). There exists an Õ(n3 max{1, log(1/ε)}) determin-
istic algorithm that given m × n matrix A with m ≤ n and ε > 0 computes a partial (1 + ε)-SVD U of A.


                                                          23
   Using the randomization technique of Diakonikolas, Tzamos, and Kane [22] a sped up version of the algorithm
can be achieved.
Lemma 2.28 (Randomized strongly polynomial partial SVD). There exists an Õ(nω max{1, log(1/ε)}) randomized
algorithm that given m × n matrix A with m ≤ n and ε > 0 computes a partial (1 + ε)-SVD U of A.


3      The Wide Neighborhood Trust Region Interior Point Method
We present and explain the algorithm TRW-IPM in Section 3.1. Then, we derive wide neighborhood polarization
in Section 3.2. Section 3.3 focuses on the analysis of TRW-IPM and proving Theorem 1.3.

3.1     Description of the Algorithm
We first formally restate the wide neighborhood trust region subproblem TR∞ (B, N, ℓ, u) from Section 1.2.
Definition 3.1. Let z = (x, s) ∈ N −∞ (θ), B ∪ N = [n] be a partition and u, ℓ > 0. We define the (B, N, ℓ, u)-trust
region direction at z as the optimal solution (∆xTR , ∆sTR , ∆y TR ) of the primal and dual trust region problems

                                ∆xN                                        ∆sB
                      min 1N +                                 min 1B +
                                 xN ∞                                       sB ∞
                                 ∆xB                                         ∆sN                 (TR∞ (B, N, ℓ, u))
                  s.t. ℓ1 ≤ 1B +     ≤ u1                    s.t. ℓ1 ≤ 1N +      ≤ u1
                                  xB                                         sN
                         A∆x = 0                                A⊤ ∆y + ∆s = 0

    This can be reduced to an instance of (TR-max), hence (∆xTR , ∆sTR , ∆y TR ) can be computed by Theorem 1.10,
we deferred the statement and its proof to the end of this section.
    We now present our Wide Neighborhood Trust Region IPM in Algorithm 1. It uses two steps: PathFollow
implementing the Robust IPM as in Theorem 2.23, and WTR for the Trust Region step as in Proposition 3.9. We
repeatedly call PathFollow to decrease the normalized gap µ by a factor ϱ = (1 − θ)9 /(256n9 ). After each call,
we guess the partition B ∪ N = [n] by setting B as the set of indices where the value of xi decreased by at most a
             4
factor (1−θ)
         4n4   during this step. We then compute a Trust Region step with (B, N ), along with a corresponding step
length. If this step decreases the normalized gap by at least ϱ, we take it. Otherwise, we discard this trust region
step, and make another call to PathFollow instead.

4     The ℓ2 -Trust Region Interior Point Method
In Section 4.1, we give a formal description of the ℓ2 -Trust Region IPM algorithm. Sections 4.2 and 4.3 prove
Lemma 1.11 on near-optimality of the trust region step for long segments.

4.1    Description of the algorithm
To start with, we restate the definition of trust region direction ∆z TR = (∆xTR , ∆sTR ) for convenience.

Definition 4.1. Let z = (x, s) ∈ N 2 (β), B ∪ N = [n] be a partition and γ > 0. We define the (B, N, γ)-ℓ2 -trust
region direction at z as the optimal solution (∆xTR , ∆sTR , ∆y TR ) of the following primal and dual trust region
problem:
                                             ∆xN                            ∆sB
                                 min 1N +                      min 1B +
                                              xN                             sB
                                       ∆xB                            ∆sN                          (TR2 (B, N, γ))
                                  s.t.        ≤γ                 s.t.        ≤γ
                                        xB                             sN
                                         A∆x = 0                        A⊤ ∆y + ∆s = 0
    The trust region constraints are given in the local norms of the search direction at z. By definition, the primal
trust region direction achieves a maximal multiplicative decrease on the coordinates in N subject to the condition
that the coordinates in B can only move within the imposed trust-region radius, as measured in the local ℓ2 -norm.
The dual trust-region direction achieves the same on the dual side with the role of N and B swapped.
    The optimal solution to TR2 (B, N, γ) exists and can be uniquely defined. By strong convexity, the vectors ∆xN
(resp. ∆sB ) are always unique. If ∆xB (resp. ∆xN ) is not unique, then it follows that the primal (resp. dual)
program has an optimal solution where the trust region constraint is not binding. In this case, we pick the unique
solution among the optimal ones where ∥∆xB /xB ∥ (resp. ∥∆sN /sN ∥) is minimal; see [40] and our Definition 7.1.
We note this corresponds to solving an instance of layered least squares problem in [73].
Proposition 4.2. TR2 (B, N, γ) can be reformulated into two instances of the trust region problem (TR-2).

Proof. We only consider the primal  trust region problem. Let P ∈ Rn×n be the column permutation matrix
such that AP = AB AN . Then taking I = B, J = N , yI = ∆x                          ∆xN
                            
                                                                   γxB , yJ = 1N + xN , b = AN xN and
                                                                     B
                                
          diag (γxB )
B = AP                             , we reach the form of (TR-2).
                      diag (xN )

   Output: (x, s) ∈ N (β) satisfying µ(x, s) ≤ µ1
              0 0

                   1

Description of ℓ2 -TR-IPM algorithm. We now formally describe our ℓ2 -Trust Region Interior Point algorithm.
We take a starting iterate (x0 , s0 ) of primal and dual feasible solutions. While µ(x, s) > µ1 , we repeat the following.
First, we perform corrector steps until reaching N 2 (β). The number of these corrector steps depends on (x0 , s0 ) in
the very first iteration; in all subsequent iterations, this will be bounded as O(1) as we need to move from N 2 (82β)
back to N 2 (β).
    Once we reached (x, s) ∈ N 2 (β), the algorithm starts with computing the affine scaling direction ∆z a at z. If
it is possible to move by a step length of at least 3/4 in this affine scaling direction (checked by line 7), then we
identify the associated partition B  ez ∪ Nez = [n] and hence compute the corresponding trust region direction ∆z TR by
solving TR(B       ez , 12β). To follow, a sufficiently long trust region step length αTR ∈ (0, 1] is computed, and then a
              ez , N
trust region step z + αTR ∆z TR is taken. For such a step, αTR and ∆z TR guarantee z + αTR ∆z TR ∈ N 2 (82β). If we
cannot move by step length of 3/4 in the affine scaling direction, the algorithm instead computes an affine scaling
step length αa ∈ (0, 3/4]; again, the step z + αa ∆z a remains in N 2 (82β). We then move on to the next iteration.
    The step formulae for affine scaling and corrector step are given in Proposition 2.11. The trust region direction
is computed from solving TR(B      ez , N
                                        ez , 12β) using a strongly polynomial subroutine which we discuss in Section 7.
We give a procedure on how to compute the step lengths αa and αTR that satisfy the mentioned properties in
strongly polynomial time in Lemma 4.7 and Lemma 5.5 respectively.

4.2     ℓ2 -polarization and trust region direction
Towards proving Lemma 1.11, we now formally derive the phenomenon of ℓ2 -polarization exhibited by the long
straight line in N 2 (β) between z0 with µ(z) = µ(z0 ) = µ and µ(z1 ) = µ1 ≤ µ/4, as assumed in the Lemma. Our
ultimate goal is to analyze the trust region step with respect to the associated partition (B   ez , N
                                                                                                     ez ) at the current
iterate z. First, we show the existence of ‘polarized’ partition (B, N ) that one can derive from the ‘ideal direction’
pointing from z to the end of the assumed straight line segment z1 . We subsequently show that these two partitions
coincide (Lemma 4.6). We formally define these concepts as follows.
Definition 4.3 (Polarization and ideal direction). Suppose there exists some z0 ∈ N 2 (β) with µ(z0 ) = µ and
z1 ∈ N 2 (β) with µ(z1 ) = µ1 ≤ µ/4 such that [z1 , z0 ] ⊆ N 2 (β). We let ∆z0 = (∆x0 , ∆s0 ) := (x1 − x0 , s1 − s0 ) and



                                                                    29
define the polarized partition to be
                                                                               
                                                        ∆x0i   ∆s0i
                                   N :=       i ∈ [n] :      ≥                      , B := [n] \ N .
                                                         x0i    s0i

For any z ∈ N 2 (β) with µ(z) = µ, we define the ideal direction to be ∆z id = (∆xid , ∆sid ) := (x1 − x, s1 − s).
Further, we define the rescaled ideal direction to be
                                                                        µ
                                  ∆z Rid = (∆xRid , ∆sRid ) :=               (x1 − x, s1 − s) .                                       (32)
                                                                      µ − µ1

   We note that the rescaling normalizes ∆z id in a way that for α ∈ [0, 1], µ(z + α∆z Rid ) = µ(1 − α). We now
show that ∆z Rid is feasible to TR2 (B, N, 12β) at z, with the objective value being O(βµ1 /µ).

4.3    Near-optimality of the ℓ2 -Trust Region Step
We are now ready to establish the key component to prove Lemma 1.12, which is that the trust region direction
of TR2 (Bez , N
              ez , 12β) has O(βµ1 /µ) residual and O(β) curvature local to z (defined below). Based on this, we
can find a step length ᾱTR ∈ (0, 1] that satisfies both µ(z + ᾱTR ∆z TR ) ≤ µ1 because of the small residual, and
z + ᾱTR ∆z TR ∈ N 2 (O(β)) because of the small curvature.
    On the other hand, the exact ᾱTR requires ℓ2 -norm computation. We will make use of ∥·∥∞ ≤ ∥·∥ ≤ ∥·∥1 ≤ n∥·∥∞
and our strongly polynomial binary search procedure (Algorithm 5) to compute a step length αTR ∈ (0, 1] that is
close enough to ᾱTR . This computed αTR is used in our TR2-IPM algorithm instead. It is slightly greater than
ᾱTR to ensure µ(z + αTR ∆z TR ) ≤ µ1 while z + αTR ∆z TR ends up in a slightly larger ℓ2 -neighborhood.

Remark 4.8. In our TR2-IPM algorithm, we compute a δ-optimal solution to TR2 (B            ez , N
                                                                                                ez , 12β) (see Section 7)
and use it for our (∆xTR , ∆sTR ) in the algorithm, instead of the actual optimal solution. Hence, our (∆xTR , ∆sTR )
admits at most a (1 + δ)-factor of violation of the trust region constraint in (33), while attaining an objective value
that is at least as good as in (34). One can then verify that (38) still holds, and hence Lemma 4.7 and Lemma 1.11
remain true if we replace the actual trust region direction with our computed (∆xTR , ∆sTR ) when δ ≤ 1/64. This
is because (38) is in fact not tight for the actual trust region direction and it allows an extra multiplicative factor
of 65/64 on ∥∆x̄TR              TR
                  B ∥ and ∥∆s̄N ∥.

   Based on Lemma 5.4 and Proposition 2.10(iii), we can find a step length ᾱa ≥ α∗ while z + ᾱa ∆z a ∈ N 2 (O(β)).
On the other hand, computing the exact ᾱa is a root finding of (13). Since TR2-IPM only takes affine scaling
step when ᾱa ≤ 3/4 (checked in line 7, can be done without square root) and ᾱa is bounded below, we can employ
our strongly polynomial binary search procedure (Algorithm 5, described in Section 7.4) to compute a step length
αa ∈ (0, 3/4] that is close enough to ᾱa , which would be used in TR2-IPM instead. It is greater than ᾱa to ensure
µ(z + αa ∆z a ) ≤ µ1 while z + αa ∆z a stays in a slightly larger ℓ2 -neighborhood.

Proof of Lemma 1.12. Under the assumption of Lemma 1.12, we apply Lemma 5.4 to get α∗ 2 ∥∆xa ∆sa ∥ /µ ≤ 20β.

    We end this section and also the analysis of our TR2-IPM by proving Theorem 1.6.
                                                  2
Proof of Theorem 1.6. Let Γ : [µ1 , µ0 ] → N (β) be the piecewise linear curve that satisfies µ(Γ(µ)) = µ for all
µ ∈ [µ1 , µ0 ] with the minimum number of linear segments T = SLC(N 2 (β), µ0 , µ1 ). Each segment is either a long
or a short straight line that stays entirely in N 2 (β). Starting from any z ∈ N 2 (β) with µ(z) ∈ [µ1 , µ0 ], there exists
z0 ∈ N 2 (β) with µ(z0 ) = µ(z) on one of the straight line segments. Let us denote the endpoint on this segment
that has the smaller duality gap to be z1 with µ(z1 ) < µ(z). Then our TR2-IPM either takes a trust region step
z + αTR ∆z TR or an affine scaling step z + αa ∆z a from z. By Lemma 1.11 and 1.12, in both cases, we obtain a point
z + α∆z ∈ N 2 (82β) with µ(z + α∆z) ≤ µ(z1 ). Then, starting from z + α∆z, the algorithm keeps taking corrector
steps until a new iterate in N 2 (β) is obtained (line 3). One can verify using Proposition 2.10(i) that it takes at
most 3 corrector steps to reach z + ∈ N 2 (β) with µ(z + ) = µ(z + α∆z) ≤ µ(z1 ). Such z + is used as the new iterate.
Therefore, for each of the T straight line segments in [µ1 , µ0 ] the TR2-IPM algorithm takes 1 predictor step, which
is either a trust region or an affine scaling step, followed by at most 3 corrector steps to traverse over the segment.
Hence, the algorithm uses at most 4T steps to terminate and generates at most 4T straight line segments. By
Lemma 1.11 and 1.12 again, all the straight line between any two consecutive iterates stay in N 2 (82β).


6     Curvature inequalities
Recall the definition of the curvature κ(z1 , z) for z1 , z ∈ P × D from (3). In this section, we focus on the curvature
between central path points. Hence, for 0 ≤ η ≤ µ, we use the shorthand κ(η, µ) := κ(z(η), z(µ)), that is,
                                                      ∥(x(η) − x(µ)) (s(η) − s(µ))∥2
                                       κ(η, µ) =                √     √ 2           .                                (46)
                                                                  µ+ η
It will be useful to use the equivalent formulation
                                                       1         x(η) − x(µ) s(η) − s(µ)
                                  κ(η, µ) =           q 2                                                           (47)
                                                          η         x(µ)        s(µ)     2
                                                 1+       µ

Recall Lemma 2.15(iii), where we showed
                                                                                            
                                   x̄s̄
                κ (η, µ) = sup          − 1n : z̄ = (x̄, s̄) = λz(η) + (1 − λ)z(µ), 0 ≤ λ ≤ 1 .                       (48)
                                  µ(z̄)     2

A natural definition for this section is therefore the following:


                                                                 38
Definition 6.1 (Path complexity). For µ, κ > 0 and K ∈ Z+ , we define SLC(η, µ, κ) to be the smallest number K
such that there exists as sequence η = ν0 ≤ · · · ≤ νk = µ such that consecutive points have curvature at most κ.
That is,                                                                                     
                 SLC(κ, η, µ) := min K : ∃(ν0 , . . . , νK ) : ν0 = η, νK = µ, max κ(νi+1 , νi ) ≤ κ   .          (49)
                                                                            i∈[K−1]

    SLC(κ, η, µ) essentially captures with three parameters how many segments we require to go down the path from
parameter µ to η while only moving from one central path point to another and never leaving the κ-neighborhood
of the central path.
    The central theorem of this section is the following:
Theorem 6.2 (Universal Curvature Theorem). There exists a universal constant C such that for any 0 ≤ η ≤ µ
with κ(η, µ) ≤ 2−8 we have that for any κ̄ ≤ κ(η, µ)
                                                                   C !
                                                            κ(η, µ)
                                       SLC (κ̄, η, µ) ≤ O                .                             (50)
                                                              κ̄

Corollary 6.3. There exists a universal constant C such that for any κ̄ ≤ κ ≤ 2−8 we have that
                                                                      
                                                       κ C
                                  SLC (κ̄, η, µ) ≤ O        SLC (κ, η, µ) .                                       (51)
                                                       κ̄

     We will prove Theorem 6.2 in the following two sections (Sections 6.1 and 6.2). The proof strategy in both
is remarkably different, however the case distinction is comparable to the optimality proof of the trust region
algorithm in previous sections. In Section 6.1 we will show that Theorem 6.2 holds for a single step whenever
η = Ω(1). Intuitively, what we will exploit there is that the denominator in the norm term in the definition of
curvature is large enough. The main idea is that if we start at (x(1), s(1)) and consider the convex combination
(x̄, s̄) of (x(1), s(1)) and x((η), s(η)) with parameter µ ∈ (η, 1), then Gonzaga’s lemma (Lemma 2.17) allows us to
bound the distance of (x̄, s̄) to the central path point (x(µ), s(µ)) in terms of the distance of (x(η), s(η)) to the
central path point (x(1), s(1)). This proximity will allow us to prove the main theorem Theorem 6.4 of the next
section.
     In Section 6.2 we use a very different strategy. There, we exploit that for the very long segments, i.e., η =
o(1) we have that the curvature κ(η, 1) is well-approximated by terms ∥(x(η)B − x(1)B )/x(1)B ∥2 and ∥(s(η)N −
s(1)N )/s(1)N ∥2 for some partition B ∪ N = [n]. It turns out that these two terms fulfill very useful identities, most
importantly Lemma 6.11. This again will lead us to the desired inequality Theorem 6.12 for long steps.
     We combine these two results in Theorem 6.2 and provide a proof at the end of this section.

6.1    Curvature bounds on short segments

because one can always rescale P and D accordingly. For some gaps 0 ≤ η ≤ µ ≤ 1 we let α = 1−µ    1−η . We further
define x̄ = αx(η) + (1 − α)x(1) and s̄ = αs(η) + (1 − α)s(1). Then (x̄, s̄) have by linearity of gap a normalized
optimality gap of µ (Proposition 2.4).

that κ(µ, ν) ≤ 24 κ(µ, 1), which gives the desired bound.

   The above theory shows that we can bound the number of steps as a function of κ/κ̄, whenever η is not too
small. In the following section, we extend the analysis to arbitrarily small η.


                                                                 42
6.2        Curvature bounds on long segments
As in the previous section fix ν = 1 and let η ≤ µ ≤ 1. Fix a partition B ∪ N = [n] and define the vector
                                                                           
                                                  x(η)B         s(η)N
                                      ϕ(η, µ) =          − 1B ,        − 1N                                                 (62)
                                                  x(µ)B         s(µ)N
    We do not identify the chosen partition in the definition of ϕ as we will throughout use the partition B ∪ N = [n]
for which ϕ(η, µ) ≪ 1, which for η ≪ µ will be unique if it exists. In particular, it will always be clear from context.
    The motivation to introduce the vector ϕ(η, µ) is multifold. Firstly, it is exactly the term whose ℓ2 -norm one
aims to bound in the trust region program. While the current version of the trust region program solves a program
on B and N separately, it clearly approximates the ℓ2 -norm within a factor of 2. Furthermore, we will show that if
η ≪ µ and the partition B ∪ N is chosen correctly, then up to scaling the norm of ϕ(η, µ) is exactly the curvature
κ(η, µ). Secondly, we are able to show strong triangle-type inequalities for the norm of the vectors ϕ(η, µ), ϕ(µ, 1)
and ϕ(η, 1), which will allow us to derive the analogue to Theorem 6.4 when the gap η ≪ 1 is small.
    We will now show the first property, that ϕ(η, µ) is a good approximation of the curvature κ(η, µ) up to scaling.

6.3     Proof of the main theorem
In this section, we prove Theorem 1.7 based on Theorem 6.2, and hence derive our main theorem (Theorem 1.4).
In the first step, we need to relate the quantity SLC(β, µ1 , µ0 ) to the straight line complexity SLC(N 2 (β), µ1 , µ0 ).
According to Definition 6.1 and Lemma 2.15(iii), SLC(β, µ1 , µ0 ) is the minimum number of pieces of a piece-
wise linear curve in N 2 (β) between µ1 and µ0 that has all breakpoints on the central path. Thus, by definition
SLC(N 2 (β), µ1 , µ0 ) ≤ SLC(β, µ1 , µ0 ). We now show the approximate reverse direction as follows.
Lemma 6.13 (Path stability). For β ∈ (0, 1/128], let z = (x, s) ∈ N 2 (β) with µ(z) = µ, z1 = (x1 , s1 ) ∈ N 2 (β)
with µ(z1 ) = µ1 ≤ µ and [z1 , z] ⊆ N 2 (β). Then we have [z(µ1 ), z(µ)] ⊆ N 2 (8β). Consequently,
                                     SLC(8β, µ1 , µ0 ) ≤ SLC(N 2 (β), µ1 , µ0 ) .

   We end this section by proving our main theorem, showing that the TR2-IPM is indeed optimal in N 2 (β).
Proof of Theorem 1.4. Recall that in order to obtain a path-following method in N 2 (β̄), we use TR2-IPM for
β = β̄/82. Given a starting z0 ∈ N 2 (β̄) = N 2 (82β) with µ(z0 ) ∈ [µ1 , µ0 ], it takes O(1) many corrector steps to
reach z0′ ∈ N 2 (β) with µ(z0′ ) = µ(z0 ). Using Theorem 1.6, TR2-IPM takes at most SLC(N 2 (β), µ1 , µ0 ) many
iterations to reach z1 ∈ N 2 (β) with µ(z1 ) ≤ µ1 and all iterates and the line segments between consecutive iterates
stay in N 2 (β̄). Then, the theorem follows from SLC(N 2 (β), µ1 , µ0 ) = O(SLC(N 2 (β̄), µ1 , µ0 )) by Theorem 1.7.
Finally, the running time bound of each iteration follows by Theorem 1.9 and Proposition 2.11.

7     Solving the ℓ2 -Trust Region Problem
We restate the ℓ2 -trust region problem (TR-2) here for convenience.

                                         min ∥yJ ∥2   s.t.   By = b, ∥yI ∥2 ≤ 1,                                 (TR-2)

We will explain its relation to the general trust region problem and its applications in Section 7.5. Our main goal of
this section is to prove Theorem 1.9. We recall that y ∈ Rn is a δ-optimal solution of (TR-2) if it satisfies By = b,
∥yI ∥2 < 1 + δ and ∥yJ ∥2 ≤ OPT2 .
Theorem 1.9. For δ ∈ (0, 1), there exists an algorithm TR2-Solve(B, b, I, J, δ) that finds a δ-optimal solution
to (TR-2) or certifies infeasibility in strongly polynomial time. Let n′ := min{|I|, |J|}. The number of arith-
                                            ′
metic operations
             is dominated by O (log (n ) + log log (|I|/δ)) many linear system solves of size n, plus the time to
              ′
get a O 22n       multiplicative approximation of the eigenvalues of a positive semidefinite matrix of size n′ . Fur-
ther, TR2-Solve(B, b, I, J, δ) can be implemented in randomized O(nω (log(n′ ) + log log(|I|/δ)) or in deterministic
      ′ 3        ′
           ω
                                      
O (n ) + n (log(n ) + log log(|I|/δ)) time.

7.1    The Lagrangian
For the description and analysis of TR2-Solve(B, b, I, J, δ), we introduce the following potential function.
Definition 7.1 (The Lagrangian). Let B ∈ Rm×n with rk(B) = m, b ∈ Rm and I ∪ J = [n] be a partition. For
λ > 0, we define
                                     y(λ) := arg min ∥yJ ∥2 + λ∥yI ∥2 .
                                                      By=b

We extend y(.) to the limit cases λ = 0 and λ = ∞ as follows. Let LJ := arg min ∥yJ ∥2 and LI := arg min ∥yI ∥2 ,
                                                                                   By=b                     By=b
and we define
                                  y(0) := arg min ∥yI ∥2 ,     y(∞) := arg min ∥yJ ∥2 .                            (72)
                                              y∈LJ                         y∈LI

For λ ∈ R≥0 ∪ {∞}, we denote ψ(λ) := ∥yI (λ)∥2 .
   Note that (TR-2) is feasible if and only if ψ(∞) ≤ 1 because ψ(∞) = miny:By=b ∥yI ∥2 , hence the feasibility of
(TR-2) can be checked by computing y(∞). From now on we assume that ψ(∞) ≤ 1. Further, if ψ(0) ≤ 1 then
y(0) is clearly an optimal solution to (TR-2). The next lemma asserts the correctness of the definition in (72) and
the strongly polynomial computability of y(0) and y(∞).
Lemma 7.2. The definitions of y(0) and ψ(0), y(∞) and ψ(∞) give continuous extensions of y(λ) and ψ(λ) at
λ = 0 and λ = ∞ respectively. The vectors y(0) and y(∞) can be computed via linear system solves.
Proof. The first half of the result follows from [40, Lemma 2.10]. Since both y(0) and y(∞) in (72) correspond to
the solutions of two layered least square problems as in [73], they can be formulated as solving linear systems of
equations. See Lemma 5.2 of [40] and Section 4.1 of [46].
    We will prove the following monotonicity properties; the proof follows from the form of ψ(λ) derived in (74).
Lemma 7.3. ψ(λ) is strictly convex and strictly decreasing on R≥0 . For any 0 < λ1 < λ2 , λ21 ψ(λ1 ) < λ22 ψ(λ2 ).
   Throughout, we let λ∗ denote the unique value such that ψ(λ∗ ) = 1. Thus, y(λ∗ ) is optimal to (TR-2) by
Lagrange duality. Our TR2-Solve(B, b, I, J, δ) algorithm essentially searches for λ such that 1 ≤ ψ(λ) < 1 + δ.
Then it follows that y(λ) is a δ-optimal solution, as we will show at the end of Section 7.3.
   Let D(λ) ∈ Rn×n be the diagonal matrix with entries D(λ)ii = 1 for any i ∈ I and D(λ)ii = λ for any i ∈ J.

   The first ingredient of our algorithm is formulated in the following lemma.
Lemma 7.5. There exists an algorithm, given in HybridNewton(λ1 , λ2 , δ), such that given δ > 0 and 0 < λ1 < λ2
with ψ(λ1 ) ≥ 1 ≥ ψ(λ2 ),          a value λ ∈ [λ1, λ2 ] such that ψ(λ) ∈ [1, 1 + δ). The algorithm runs in strongly

dominated by O(1) linear system solves of size min{m, |I|, |J|}.
    We describe this subroutine in Section 7.4, which follows a similar approach of standard hybrid Newton’s method
in the literature, see e.g. [15, 77]. It first performs a geometric binary search to narrow down the search interval by
finding λ̂ ∈ [λ1 , λ2 ) with ψ(λ̂) ≥ 1 ≥ ψ(1.1λ̂). Starting from λ̂, we then perform Newton’s method for root finding
of ψ(λ) = 1, which exhibits quadratic convergence as now we start sufficiently close to λ∗ .

7.2    The critical points

that λ2 /λ1 = 2poly(n) /poly(δ) and then applying HybridNewton(λ1 , λ2 , δ) as in Lemma 7.5.

   We are now ready to describe how we compute in strongly polynomial time a sequence of approximate critical
points that are multiplicatively close to the actual critical points.
Lemma 7.10. For ϱ > 1, it takes one pivoted Cholesky factorization of size min{|I|, |J|} to compute ϱ-approximate
eigenvalues (β̂k )k∈P such that ϱ−1 β̂k ≤ βk ≤ ϱβ̂k for critical points (βk )k∈P .
Proof. If |I| ≥ |J|, we compute the pivoted Cholesky factorization of M1 as in Lemma C.1, which gives approximate
eigenvalues (β̂k )k∈P such that ϱ−1 β̂k ≤ βk ≤ ϱβ̂k by Corollary C.5. If |J| ≥ |I| we compute the pivoted Cholesky
factorization of M2 instead which gives approximate eigenvalues (1/β̂k )∈P such that ϱ−1 β̂k ≤ βk ≤ ϱβ̂k . Hence,
the overall running time is dominated by one pivoted Cholesky factorization for a matrix of size min{|I|, |J|}.
Remark 7.11. We use ϱ to denote the approximation factor from computing the eigenvalues to allow the use of
algorithms other than the pivoted Cholesky factorization that give better multiplicative approximation.

7.3        The TR-Solve Algorithm
In this subsection, we fully describe our TR2-Solve(B, b, I, J, δ) algorithm; the pseudocode is given in Algorithm 3.
We explain how the algorithm finds a δ-optimal solution to (TR-2) and also the number of arithmetic operations it
needs in strongly polynomial time, and hence prove Theorem 1.9.
    The TR2-Solve(B, b, I, J, δ) algorithm first checks the values ψ(∞) and ψ(0). When ψ(∞) > 1, (TR-2) is
infeasible. When ψ(0) < 1 + δ, we return y(0) as a δ-optimal solution to (TR-2). For the rest of the algorithm,
ψ(0) ≥ 1 + δ and ψ(∞) ≤ 1. We compute the approximate critical points β̂k as in Lemma 7.10, and let P ∗ include
0, ∞, and ν β̂k /ϱ, ϱβ̂k /ν for all the β̂k computed; we set ν = δ/4. We then find consecutive λ1 < λ2 in P ∗ such
that ψ(λ1 ) ≥ 1 ≥ ψ(λ2 ). If ψ(λ1 ) < 1 + δ, we can terminate with y = y(λ1 ). If λ2 ≤ ϱ2 λ1 /ν 2 i.e. λ1 and λ2 are

 Algorithm 3: TR2-Solve(B, b, I, J, δ)

   Output: a δ-optimal solution y ∈ Rn to (TR-2) or infeasibility conclusion.
 1 Compute y(∞) and y(0) ;
 2 if ψ(∞) > 1 then return Infeasible and Terminate;
 3 if ψ(0) < 1 + δ then return y = y(0) and Terminate;
 4 ν ← δ/4 ;

 5 Compute the ϱ-approximate critical points (β̂k )k∈P according to Lemma 7.10;

             ν β̂k ϱβ̂k

                         ∗

‘close’, HybridNewton(λ1 , λ2 , δ) is called, and Lemma 7.5 asserts that it returns a value λ with 1 ≤ ψ(λ) < 1 + δ
in strongly polynomial time.
    The remaining case is when λ2 > ϱ2 λ1 /ν 2 i.e. λ1 and λ2 are far away. We then define new values λ̂1 and λ̂2 in
line 11. Clearly, λ̂2 /λ̂1 ≤ 4|I|/δ. The next two lemmas guarantee that ψ(λ̂1 ) > 1 and ψ(λ̂2 ) < 1 + δ. Therefore, we
can again call HybridNewton(λ1 , λ2 , δ) on [λ̂1 , λ̂2 ] to find a value λ with 1 ≤ ψ(λ) < 1 + δ.
    The key part of the analysis is based on that no critical points can lie between λ1 ν and λ2 /ν by the construction
of P ∗ . This enables us to approximate ψ(λ) with a simpler function p + q/λ2 on [λ1 , λ2 ].

Proof of Theorem 1.9. TR2-Solve(B, b, I, J, δ) algorithm either returns Infeasible when ψ(∞) > 1, or y = y(λ)
for some λ ≥ 0. We first claim that λ1 > 0 in the algorithm. Indeed, if λ1 = 0, no (βk )k∈P can lie between λ1 ν
and λ2 /ν. This gives a contradiction to Lemma 7.13. Hence, y(0) being returned can only occur in line 3, and it is
clearly δ-optimal to (TR-2) in this case.



                                                               52
   If λ > 0 is returned, then 1 ≤ ψ(λ) < 1 + δ by Lemma 7.5. Hence, y(λ) is δ-feasible to (TR-2) with ∥yI (λ)∥2 ≥ 1.
Let y ∗ denote the optimal solution of (TR-2). Then, by the optimality of y(λ) and feasibility of y ∗ ,

                             ∥yJ (λ)∥2 + λ ≤ ∥yJ (λ)∥2 + λ∥yI (λ)∥2 ≤ ∥yJ∗ ∥2 + λ∥yI∗ ∥2 ≤ OPT2 + λ                                                (76)

which gives ∥yJ (λ)∥2 ≤ OPT2 , and hence y(λ) is a δ-optimal solution to (TR-2).
    ψ(λ) is always computed via linear system solve by Lemma 7.4. Hence, Lemma 7.5 and Lemma C.1 together
shows that TR2-Solve(B, b, I, J, δ) runs in strongly polynomial time. In particular, HybridNewton(λ1 , λ2 , δ)
is conducted either in the interval [λ1 , λ2 ] with λ2 ≤ ϱ2 λ1 /ν 2 which requires O (log log (ϱ/δ)) many iteration, or
in the interval [λ̂1 , λ̂2 ] with λ̂2 /λ̂1 ≤ 4|I|/δ which requires O (log log (|I|/δ)) many iteration. We always have
ϱ ≤ min{|I|, |J|}22 min{|I|,|J|} because of Corollary C.5 for the pivoted Cholesky factorization. Hence, the running
time stated in Theorem 1.9 follows.

7.4     The HybridNewton subroutine
In this subsection, we present the subroutine HybridNewton(λ1 , λ2 , δ) and prove Lemma 7.5. Recall that for a
general function f ∈ C 2 (R), if an interval [λ1 , λ2 ] such that f (λ1 ) ≥ f (λ2 ) and an initial guess λ0 ∈ [λ1 , λ2 ] are
given, Newton’s method for root finding computes the iterates by

                                                                       f (λt )

until a certain convergence criterion is met. The main part of our subroutine is to apply Newton’s method to
ψ(λ) − 1 = 0 in an interval [λ1 , λ2 ] that satisfies ψ(λ1 ) ≥ 1 + δ and ψ(λ2 ) ≤ 1. We first prove that ψ ′ on R>0 can be
computed by solving a linear system, so that Newton’s method can be implemented in strongly polynomial time.

   We note that both log(−ψ ′ ) and log(ψ ′′ ) are Lipschitz continuous on R>0 , showing the multiplicative stability

   As in [77], we safeguard Newton’s method with an initial guess λ0 ∈ [λ1 , λ∗ ) that satisfies λ∗ ≤ 1.1λ0 using a
binary search on [λ1 , λ2 ]. We give the following binary search subroutine for root finding on a bounded interval,
which performs a geometric binary search without taking square roots.
                                                                                       
Lemma 7.16. BinarySearch(f, λ1 , λ2 , ε) is correct and takes O log log λλ21 + log 1ε        many operations.
                                                                                  N
Proof. Since f is decreasing on [λ1 , λ2 (1+ε)], f (λ1 (1+ε)2 ) ≤ f (λ2 ) ≤ 0. Then, by the construction of λ̂, f (λ̂) > 0,
λ̂ ≥ λ1 , and also λ̂ < λ2 by the monotonicity of f . Also, λ̂ satisfies f (λ̂(1 + ε)) ≤ 0 by the termination condition.


                                                                              54
 Algorithm 5: BinarySearch(f, λ1 , λ2 , ε)
   Input : 0 < λ1 < λ2 with f (λ1 ) > 0 ≥ f (λ2 ), ε ∈ (0, 1), f a decreasing function on [λ1 , λ2 (1 + ε)].
   Output: λ̂ ∈ [λ1 , λ2 ] such that f (λ̂) > 0 ≥ f (λ̂(1 + ε))
 1 N ← 0;
                    2N
 2 while λ1 (1 + ε)    < λ2 do N ← N + 1;
                  N
 3 L ← 0, R ← 2 ;
 4 while R > L + 1 do
 5    M ← L+R   2 ;
 6    if f (λ1 (1 + ε)M ) > 0 then L ← M ;
 7    else R ← M ;
 8   return λ̂ = λ1 (1 + ε)L


It takes O (log log (λ2 /λ1 ) + log (1/ε)) to find the desirable N and then the algorithm conducts a binary search in
some subset of {0, 1, ..., 2N }, which shows the running time.
    For computational efficiency, we also need that L and R remain integer throughout. This follows since R − L is
divided by 2 in each iteration. The initial value is R − L = 2N , and hence remains an integer power of 2 throughout.
This also shows that BinarySearch(f, λ1 , λ2 , ε) can be implemented in strongly polynomial time.

     Finally, we conclude this section by proving the correctness and running time of HybridNewton(λ1 , λ2 , δ):
Proof of Lemma 7.5. HybridNewton(λ1 , λ2 , δ) first calls BinarySearch (ψ, λ1 , λ2 , 0.1) to find λ0 ∈ [λ1 , λ2 ] with
ψ(λ0 ) > 1 ≥ ψ(1.1λ0 ), taking O(log log(λ2 /λ1 )) many iterations. By the monotonicity of ψ, λ0 < λ∗ ≤ 1.1λ0 . Then
by Lemma 7.15, λt ∈ [λ0 , λ∗ ) with 1 ≤ ψ(λt ) < 1 + δ is returned by the subsequent Newton’s method starting at
λ0 in log log (4/δ) many iterations.
    In each iteration, HybridNewton(λ1 , λ2 , δ) at most computes ψ(λt ) by Lemma 7.4 and ψ ′ (λt ) by Lemma 7.14
for a fixed λt ∈ [λ1 , λ2 ], which amounts to O(1) linear system solves of size m. As a result, we have the strongly
polynomial computability. We can adopt Woodbury matrix identity to compute ψ(λt+1 ) and ψ ′ (λt+1 ) based on
the previous iterate ψ(λt ) and ψ ′ (λt ) in HybridNewton(λ1 , λ2 , δ). One can verify that now we have O(1) linear
system solves of size min{m, |I|, |J|} in each iteration. This results in the stated running time in Lemma 7.5.

7.5     General Trust Region Problems
In this section, we consider the general trust region problems in e.g. [15, 28, 54] and explain their relation to (TR-2)
and the applicability of our methods.
    Before that, let us mention that the standard ridge regression problem can be seen as a special case of (TR-2).
Given a matrix X ∈ Rm×k and b ∈ Rm , where the columns represent regressor variables and b the dependent
variable, and the rows represent m observations. For some ‘tuning parameter’ λ ≥ 0, the goal is to find a coefficient
vector β ∈ Rk+1 , indexed from 0 to k, such that
                                                                           2
                                              m
                                              X             k
                                                            X                          k
                                                                                       X
                                β = arg min         β0 +         βj Xi,j − bi  + λ         βi2 .
                                              i=1           j=1                        j=1

                                                                                             Pk
This can be equivalently written as a trust region problem, with a trust region constraint j=1 βi2 ≤ t2 . These two
forms are used interchangeably in the statistical literature, see e.g., [34, Section 3.4.1]. Our algorithm shows how
to find the λ corresponding (up to a small error) to the given trust region constraint in strongly polynomial time.
    The above can be easily encoded in the form (TR-2) with
                                                                    
                                               B = 1m X −Im ,

where the first k + 1 coordinates represent β, and the last m coordinates the regression error. We set I as the
coordinates corresponding to X and J as the coordinates in In . Note that the first coordinate representing β0 does
not belong to either set; this column can be easily eliminated using row operations, and thus we obtain the form
(TR-2).



                                                             55
    We now turn to the general ℓ2 -trust region problem in the form
                                                   min ⟨x, Hx⟩ + ⟨c, x⟩
                                                                                                                 (80)
                                                    ∥Mx − a∥ ≤ 1 .

For c ∈ Rn , H ∈ Rn×n , M ∈ Rk×n , a ∈ Rk . In general, H ∈ Rn×n is not assumed to be positive semidefinite, and
thus, the problem is not convex. Surprisingly, the problem is polynomial-time solvable even in this case, as shown
by Ye [77]. There are also various numerical algorithms e.g., [28].
   We show that under the following assumption, (80) is solvable in strongly polynomial time using our algorithm.
                                            inf ⟨x, Hx⟩ + ⟨c, x⟩ > −∞ ,                                          (81)
                                            x∈Rn

that is, the unconstrained minimum value of the objective in (80) is bounded from below. This is equivalent to
assuming H ⪰ 0 and c ∈ Im(H).
     Let us consider LDLT factorization H = UDU⊤ , where D a nonnegative diagonal matrix with diagonal entries
di . This can be computed in O(n3 ) using Cholesky factorization. By the assumption c ∈ Im(H), it follows that
 c, U−⊤
      ·,i  = 0 whenever di = 0.
     We define y ∈ R2n with a partition [2n] = I ∪ J into two sets of n indices, such that
                                                                    1
                                     yI = Mx − a ,       yJ = U⊤ x + D† U−1 c .
                                                                    2
This substitution can be encoded in the linear system By = b, where
                                                                       1
                                            MU−⊤ ,             b := a + MU−⊤ D† U−1 c ,
                                                
                               B := −In
                                                                       2
and the two blocks represent the sets I and J. The objective can be written as
                                                    X         1
                                 ⟨x, Hx⟩ + ⟨c, x⟩ =   di yi2 − c⊤ U−⊤ D† U−1 c .
                                                              4
                                                       i∈J

After removing the constant term, (80) is equivalent to
                                                     X
                                                min     di yi2
                                                         i∈J
                                                                                                                 (82)
                                                             ∥yI ∥2 ≤ 1
                                                        By = b .

This form differs from (TR-2) because ∥yJ ∥2 is replaced by weighted squares. Note that if we were able to compute
their square roots, then weights could be encoded in the matrix B. Without square root computations, we need to
keep this more general form. However,   the algorithm described in this section can be used essentially unchanged.
We define y(λ) = arg minBy=b i∈J di yi2 + λ∥yI ∥2 , and ψ(λ) = ∥yI (λ)∥2 . We can compute y(λ) by solving a linear
                               P
                                                                                                              √
system as before; and since the problem is equivalent to the setting where we rescale the coordinates in J by di ,
all properties remain unchanged.


8     Solving the ℓ∞ -Trust Region Problem
In this section we provide a proof to Theorem 1.10 from the introduction by describing how to solve the program
(TR-max), which we restate here for convenience.
                                      min ∥yJ ∥∞      s.t.   By = b , ∥yI ∥∞ ≤ 1 .                        (TR-max)
Our goal is to find a δ-optimal solution as in Definition 1.8. We will throughout assume that b ∈ Im(B) as otherwise
infeasibility is trivial to prove through a projection in time O(nω ). Throughout this section, the input (B, b, I, J)
as well as the required accuracy δ > 0 will be fixed. For convenience, we assume δ ≤ 1/n . We let λ⋆ denote the
optimum value of (TR-max).
     Recall the definition of an approximate ℓ∞ -regression solver from
                                                                      Definition 2.24 and the subroutine LMFeas(B, b, δ)
from Theorem 2.25 implementing the solver in time nω̃+o(1) log 1δ . We can use this to check feasibility of (TR-max),
and also to make approximate queries on λ⋆ . To state this subroutine, we first need to define the appropriate cer-
tificates.


                                                             56
Definition 8.1 (U ε -certificates and L-certificates). Let ε ≥ 0, and λ ∈ R≥0 ∪ {∞}. We say that y ∈ Rm is an
U ε -certificate for λ if By = b, ∥yJ ∥∞ ≤ (1 + ε)λ, ∥yI ∥∞ ≤ 1 + ε. We say that z ∈ Rm is an L-certificate for λ if it
is a Farkas certificate showing that By = b, ∥yJ ∥∞ ≤ λ, ∥yI ∥∞ ≤ 1 is infeasible. Namely,
  (i) ∥B⊤
        I z∥1 < ⟨b, z⟩ if λ = 0;

 (ii) ∥B⊤          ⊤
        I z∥1 + λ∥BJ z∥1 < ⟨b, z⟩ if 0 < λ < ∞;

(iii) B⊤              ⊤
       J z = 0J and ∥BI z∥1 < ⟨b, z⟩ if λ = ∞.

Further, let U ε ⊆ R≥0 ∪ {∞} be the set of λ values for which a U ε -certificate exists, and let L ⊆ R≥0 ∪ {∞} for
which an L-certificate exists.
   Clearly, U ε ∪ L = R≥0 ∪ {∞}. Note that if (TR-max) is feasible, then L = [0, λ⋆ ), and U ε = [α, ∞) for some
α ≤ λ⋆ . However, these sets may overlap: α < λ⋆ is possible.
Lemma 8.2. Given (B, b, I, J), there exists a subroutine LMGuess(λ,       ε) that, for given λ ∈ R≥0 ∪ {∞}, either
returns a U ε -certificate or an L-certificate for λ in nω̃+o(1) log 1ε .
                                                                       

Proof. If λ = 0, we simply call LMFeas(BI , b, ε). If 0 < λ < ∞, then let u ∈ Rn be defined by uI = 1I and
uJ = λ1J . Then, LMFeas(Bdiag (u) , b, ε) yields the desired outcome. For the feasibility case λ = ∞, we run
LMFeas(B′ , b′ , ε) where B′ = (Im − BJ B†J )BI and b′ = (Im − BJ B†J )b. Note that B′ y ′ = b′ for y ′ ∈ RI if and
only if there exists y ′′ ∈ RJ such that B(y ′ , y ′′ ) = b; we can obtain such a solution as y ′′ = B†J (b − BI y ′ ).
   Assume we have bounds 0 < λ1 < λ2 with the guarantees λ1 ∈ L and λ2 ∈ U δ/4 . Then, we can use a binary
search subroutine, the same way as in BinarySearch in Section 7.4 to find a δ-optimal solution to (TR-max) in
O(log log(λ2 /λ1 ) + log(1/δ)) calls to LMGuess. Namely, we find values λ′ ≤ λ′′ ≤ (1 + δ/4)λ′ such that λ′ ∈ L
and λ′′ ∈ U δ/4 . We summarize this in the following lemma:
Lemma 8.3. Given (B, b, I, J), there exists a subroutine LM-Binary(λ1 , λ2 , δ) that, for given 0 < λ1 < λ2 such
                                                                                       1
                                                                                                    λ2         1
                                                                                                                  
that λ1 ∈ L and λ2 ∈ U δ/4 , returns a δ-optimal solution to (TR-max) in time nω̃+o(1) log
                                                                                       δ    log log  λ1 + log δ      .

    Our ℓ∞ -Trust Region solver follows a similar principle as the ℓ2 -Trust Region solver in Section 7. We identify
critical points based on an approximate singular value computation. Using binary search, we can narrow down the
search interval to [λ1 , λ2 ] with λ1 ∈ L and λ2 ∈ U δ/4 so that every critical value is either much larger than λ2 or
much smaller than λ1 . If λ2 /λ1 is polynomially bounded, we call LM-Binary(λ1 , λ2 , δ). If λ2 ≫ λ1 , then we use
the approximate feasible solution computed at λ2 to further narrow down the search space to [λ̂1 , λ̂2 ] ⊆ [λ1 , λ2 ]
with λ̂2 /λ̂1 = O(n), and call LM-Binary(λ̂1 , λ̂2 , δ).

8.1    Critical points from the lifting operator
We obtain critical values from the lifting operator L ∈ RI×J defined as
                                                                            † 
                           L := B†I BJ IJ − B⊤  J   I m − B B†
                                                           I I   B ⊤
                                                                   J   Im − B B†
                                                                             I I       .                           (83)

   If for a vector y ∈ RJ , there exists y ′ ∈ RI such that BJ y = BI y ′ , then y ′ = Ly is the unique minimum norm
such vector. To better understand this map, let us decompose RJ = W1 ⊕ W2 ⊕ W3 , where

             W1 = ker(BJ ) ,
             W2 = {y ∈ RJ : y ⊥ ker(BJ ), BJ y ∈ Im(BI )} = {y ∈ RJ : BJ y ∈ Im(BI ), y ∈ Im(B⊤
                                                                                              J )} ,

             W3 = {y ∈ RJ : y ⊥ ker((Im − BI B†I )BJ )} = {y ∈ RJ : y ∈ Im(B⊤           †
                                                                            J (Im − BI BI ))} .

                                                                                   †
                                                                  †                  †
Note that ker(L) = W1 ⊕W3 . Further, note that IJ −B⊤
                                                    J   I m − BI BI   B⊤
                                                                       J   I m − BI BI     is the projection onto
ker((Im − BI B†I )BJ ) and therefore acts as identity on W2 , and therefore Lw2 = B†I BJ w2 for any w2 ∈ W2 . For
y = (yI , yJ ) with an orthogonal decomposition yJ = w1 + w2 + w3 , wi ∈ Wi , i = 1, 2, 3, we have that for y ′ := LyJ
the equality y ′ = Lw2 = B+                    ′
                            I BJ w2 and so BI y = BJ w2 holds. As such By = BI yI +BJ yJ = BI yI +BJ (w2 +w3 ) =
BI (yI + y ) + BJ w3 = B(yI + y , w3 ). The vector (yI + y ′ , w3 ) gives the largest possible reduction ℓ2 -norm on the
            ′                     ′

coordinates in J while preserving By; w3 is the part that cannot be eliminated.


                                                          57
   For some factor ϱ ≥ 1, we compute a ϱ-approximate partial SVD U ∈ Rrk(L)×J (see Definition 2.26) of L
using Lemma 2.27 or Lemma 2.28. We then compute the approximate lengths σi ∈ ∥Ui,: ∥2 [0.5, 2] according to
Proposition 2.1. Let us assume these are ordered decreasingly as σ1 ≥ · · · ≥ σrk(L) > 0. These will be the critical
points in the algorithm.

8.2    The algorithm
The algorithm TRW-Solve(B, b, I, J, δ) is shown as Algorithm 6. We start by calls to LMGuess(∞, δ) to first
determine approximate feasibility of (TR-max) and then LMGuess(0, δ) to determine whether an approximately
feasible solution with objective value 0 exists.
    If we conclude that the problem (TR-max) is approximately feasible but the objective value is strictly positive,
we proceed by computing a ϱ-approximate partial SVD U ∈ Rrk(L)×J of the lifting operator L; let u(i) ∈ RJ denote
the i-th row of U. Let ℓ = rk(L) and σ1 ≥ . . . ≥ σℓ > 0 be 2-approximations of the ∥u(i) ∥2 norms, ordered
decreasingly.
    We define the breakpoints P ⋆ for the algorithm to include 0, ∞, and for each k ∈ [ℓ], the values δ/(4ϱnσk ) and
4ϱn/(δσk ). We then perform binary search on P ⋆ , making calls to LMGuess(λ, δ/4) to find two consecutive values
λ1 < λ2 such that λ1 ∈ L and λ2 ∈ U δ/4 .
    If λ2 /λ1 ≤ 64ϱ2 n2 /δ 2 , then we can find a δ/4-approximate optimal solution y using LM-Binary(λ1 , λ2 , δ/4).
If the gap λ2 /λ1 is above this threshold, i.e., λ2 /λ1 > 64ϱ2 n2 /δ 2 , then let k ∈ [ℓ] be the smallest value such that
σk < 1/λ2 . By construction,

                                δ                                          σj−1   σk−1   2ϱn
           ∥u(j) ∥2 ≤ 2σk <         ,   ∀k ≤ j ≤ ℓ ,    and ∥u(j) ∥2 ≥          ≥      ≥     ,      ∀1 ≤ j ≤ k − 1 .   (84)
                              2ϱnλ2                                          2      2    δλ1
By definition of the ϱ-approximate SVD, this in particular means that

           u(j)                 δ                                      u(j)   ∥u(j) ∥2   2n

          ∥u ∥2               2nλ2                                    ∥u ∥2      ϱ       δλ1

that is, the directions u(j) for j ≥ k are very cheap to lift, while the directions u(j) with j < k are very expensive
to lift. The vectors {u(j) : j ∈ [ℓ]} form a basis of W2 . Let us further decompose

                  W2 = H1 ⊕ H2 ,        H1 := span{uk : 1 ≤ j ≤ k − 1} ,         H2 := span{uk : k ≤ j ≤ ℓ} ,

that is, H1 is the subspace of ‘expensive to lift’ and H2 the subspace of ‘cheap to lift’ directions. We now consider
the U δ/4 -certificate ȳ for λ2 , and modify it in two steps. First, we obtain ỹ by replacing ȳJ with B†J BJ ȳJ , i.e.,
projecting out the part in W1 = ker(BJ ). We next set

by LM-Binary( n1 λ̂, λ̂, δ).
    To justify this choice, Lemma 8.4 below shows that ŷ = (ỹI + Lz, ỹJ − z) is a U δ -certificate for λ̂. In Lemma 8.5
we then show that λ⋆ ≥ λ̂/n.

Theorem 1.10. For δ ∈ (0, 1), there exists an algorithm TRW-Solve(B, b, I, J, δ) that finds a δ-optimal solution
to (TR-max) or certifies infeasibility in strongly polynomial time. The running time of the algorithm is randomized
nω̃+o(1) log(1/δ) or deterministic nω̃+o(1) log(1/δ) + O(n3 ).
Proof. Correctness follows from Lemma 8.4 and Lemma 8.5. In particular, whenever LM-Binary(λ′ , λ′′ , δ ′ ) is
                            ′
called, λ′ ∈ L and λ′′ ∈ U δ . The running time bound follows by Lemma 8.3.

C     Strongly Polynomial Eigenvalue Approximation
In this section we provide the strongly polynomial algorithms to compute approximate eigenvalues of positive
semi-definite matrices as well as partial singular value decompositions of general matrices (see Definition 2.26 in
Section 2.8).
Lemma C.1 (Pivoted Cholesky factorization). Let M ∈ Rn×n be a positive semi-definite matrix. One can in
strongly polynomial time with O(n3 ) many arithmetic operations compute a Cholesky factorization

                                                M = PLDL⊤ P⊤ ,

where P is a permutation matrix, D = diag (di )i∈[n] and L is unit lower-triangular with all entries being bounded
by 1 in absolute value. Furthermore, for the eigenvalues (λi (M))i∈[n] of M, we have

                                           di
                                                ≤ λi (M) ≤ n2 di ,    ∀i ∈ [n] .
                                       n2 22n−2
    As a corollary of the above theorem, the trust region direction ∆z TR = (∆xTR , ∆sTR ) used in TR2-IPM can
be computed in strongly polynomial time, and hence, every iteration of TR2-IPM algorithm can be implemented
in strongly polynomial time.

C.1    A deterministic pivoted Cholesky decomposition algorithm in cubic time
In this section, we give a strongly polynomial algorithm to compute multiplicative approximations to the eigenvalues
of any positive semi-definite matrix and hence prove Lemma C.1. This algorithm is used as a subroutine in TR2-
Solve(B, b, I, J, δ) to compute the approximate critical points of ψ. In the second part of this section, we present
a randomized algorithm given in [22] and explain how it can also be used to provide the approximate eigenvalues
of a positive semi-definite matrix.
Lemma C.2 (Pivoted Cholesky via Schur Complements for PSD Matrices). Let M ∈ Rn×n be any positive semi-
definite matrix. Then there exists a factorization

                                                M = PLDL⊤ P⊤ ,

where P is a permutation matrix, D is diagonal with nonnegative entries, and L is lower-triangular with unit
diagonal entries and bounded off-diagonal elements:

                                  diag(L) = 1   and   |Lij | ≤ 1     (for all i > j).

This factorization can be computed in O(n3 ) time by selecting the largest diagonal entry as pivot at each stage and
performing symmetric row/column interchanges.
Proof. We proceed by induction on the matrix dimension. The base case n = 1 is trivial. For the inductive step,
assume the statement holds for any (n − 1) × (n − 1) positive semidefinite matrix.
                                               c let p ∈ [n] index its largest diagonal entry. Define S ∈ Rn×n as

We next show that the diagonal entries of D provide multiplicative estimates on the eigenvalues of M. For this, we
require the following two standard results.

Fact C.3 (Inverse Norm Bound of Triangular factor). Let L be an n × n lower-triangular matrix with diag(L) = 1.
Then its inverse L−1 satisfies
                                         ∥L−1 ∥∞ ≤ 2n−1 ∥L∥n−1∞ .

   In particular, if |Lij | ≤ 1 for all i > j then ∥L−1 ∥∞ ≤ 2n−1 .

Proof. The absolute value of the entry (i, j) of L−1 is given by |adj(L)i,j / det(L)|, where adj(L) is the adjugate
matrix of L. Clearly, det(L) = 1. Furthermore, adj(L)i,j is the determinant of a (n − 1) × (n − 1) submatrix L̄ of
L. Since L is a lower triangular matrix, the submatrix L̄ is a lower Hessenberg matrix with ∥L̄∥∞ ≤ ∥L∥∞ .
   Using the Leibniz formula, we can bound:

                                     X                  n−1
                                                        Y
                                                                                     n−1
                     | det(L̄)| =             sgn(τ )         L̄k,τ (k) ≤ |Sn−1 |∥L̄∥∞   ≤ (n − 1)! ∥L∥n−1
                                                                                                       ∞ ,
                                    τ ∈Sn−1             k=1


where Sn−1 is the permutation group on n − 1 elements of size (n − 1)!. However, due to the Hessenberg structure
of L̄, the number of permutations τ ∈ Sn−1 such that L̄k,τ (k) ̸= 0 for all k ∈ [n − 1] is bounded by 2n−1 . Hence,
|det(L̄)| ≤ 2n−1 ∥L∥n−1
                    ∞ .

Fact C.4. Let M ∈ Rn×n and N ∈ Rn×n be symmetric, and let R ∈ Rn×n be invertible. Let M = RNR⊤ . Then,
for any i = 1, . . . , n, we have that
                                            1
                                                  σi (N) ≤ σi (M) ≤ ∥R∥2 σi (N).
                                          ∥R−1 ∥2

have that
                                         1

                                     2

C.2     A randomized algorithm in matrix multiplication time
In this section, we show that the computations in the algorithm by Diakonikolas, Tzamos, and Kane [22] can be
modified to run in matrix multiplication time Õ(nω ). Let us first verbatim state their result.

Proposition C.6 ([22, Proposition 4.1]). Given an n × n PSD matrix M, an accuracy parameter ε > 0 and a
failure probability δ > 0, there is an algorithm (Algorithm 7) that computes orthogonal vectors q1 , . . . , qn and scalars
ai , i ∈ [n] such that the matrix Mc = Pn ai qi q ⊤ satisfies the following: for all v ∈ Rn , it holds that
                                           i=1     i


                                              |v ⊤ (M − M)v|
                                                        c    ≤ ε(v ⊤ Mv) .                                           (101)

The algorithm performs poly(n/ε, log(1/δ)) arithmetic operations on poly(n/ε, log(1/δ), b)-bit numbers, where b is
the bit complexity of the entries of M.
    We will only require the correctness statement in Proposition C.6. We need certain modifications compared to
[22], because in our computational model, we cannot operate on bits. However, we show that the algorithm can
be implemented in Õ(nω ) in the real RAM model. In particular, we will now describe how the algorithm can be
implemented in Õ(nω ) time. First, note that the t-th power of M for t = O(n6 /ε2 log(n/δ)) can be computed
in O(log t) many matrix multiplications via repeated squaring, hence Õ(nω (log(1/ε) + log log(1/δ))) overall time.
Furthermore, the size of the numbers is polynomially bounded in the input size. The other computational bottleneck
is computing the vectors qi in line 4. A naı̈ve sequential algorithm would take O(n2 ) time per call, resembling
the overall O(n3 ) complexity of the previous section, where pivoting forced us to compute the orthogonalization
sequentially. Here, however, as proven by the correctness in Proposition C.6 no pivoting is needed. The procedure
therefore corresponds to computing the QR decomposition of the matrix Mt A. This matrix has size n × n, and
so the QR decomposition can be computed in Õ(nω ) time [18, 23, 72]. This concludes the analysis that the
overall algorithm can be implemented in Õ(nω (log(1/ε) + log log(1/δ))) time. The guarantee in Proposition C.6 will
                                                                                                                    q ⊤ Mq
provide us with (1 + ϵ)-approximations to the eigenvalues of the matrix M. More precisely, the values ai = iq⊤ qi i
                                                                                                             i
approximate the eigenvalues of M within a factor of 1 ± ε. The proof is standard, nonetheless we provide it for
completeness in the appendix (Lemma C.9). Based on this discussion, we arrive at the following theorem.
Theorem C.7. Given an n × n PSD matrix M, an accuracy parameter ε > 0 and a failure probability δ > 0, there
is an algorithm (Algorithm 7) that can be implemented in Õ(nω (log(1/ϵ) + log(log(1/δ)))) time that computes a
matrix Q ∈ Rn×n = [q1 , . . . , qn ] with orthogonal columns such that for ai = qi⊤ Mqi /(qi⊤ qi ) and M
                                                                                                       c = P ai qi q ⊤ for
                                                                                                            i       i
all v ∈ Rn , it holds that
                                                |v ⊤ (M − M)v|
                                                          c    ≤ ε(v ⊤ Mv) .                                         (102)
   Furthermore, we have that (1 − ε)σi (M) ≤ ai ≤ (1 + ε)σi (M).

Lemma 2.27 (Deterministic strongly polynomial partial SVD). There exists an Õ(n3 max{1, log(1/ε)}) determin-
istic algorithm that given m × n matrix A with m ≤ n and ε > 0 computes a partial (1 + ε)-SVD U of A.

   By operator monotonicity we therefore have that
                                                    1/p                        1/p

              (QDQ⊤ )1/p = Q(Q⊤ Q)1/p−1 D1/p Q⊤ = Q−⊤ (Q⊤ Q)1/p D1/p Q⊤ = Q̄(Q⊤ Q)1/p D1/p Q̄⊤ ,                  (114)

where Q̄ is the matrix with orthonormal columns obtained by normalizing the columns of Q, so in particular
Q̄⊤ Q̄ = I and Q̄−⊤ = Q̄. Furthermore, M2/p = A⊤ A. We can therefore conclude that for all x ∈ Rn we have that

                  ϱ−1/p ∥(Q⊤ Q)1/(2p) D1/(2p) Q̄⊤ x∥22 ≤ ∥Ax∥22 ≤ ϱ1/p ∥(Q⊤ Q)1/(2p) D1/(2p) Q̄⊤ x∥22 .           (115)

above exactly. However, we can access Q exactly. By computing dˆi ≈ ∥AQ:,i ∥2 /∥Q:,i ∥2 to high multiplicative
accuracy 1 + ε/4 for i ∈ [n] we can obtain the diagonal matrix D̂ = diag (d1 , . . . , dn ) such that for all i ∈ [n] we
have that
